# The Mahler Conjecture for General Convex Bodies

Coordinate `EN-108-000-0000-2-2026-PR-T4-UK-EX-M2-B-ML-M-0192` | `PM-087.2` | status ACTIVE | original slug `The-Mahler-Conjecture-for-General-Convex-Bodies-September-22-2026` | date 2026-09-22 | derived from the openai/math preprint The-Mahler-Conjecture-for-General-Convex-Bodies-September-22-2026, modified (Apache-2.0). Publication status: DRAFT.

<!-- PM:BEGIN pm.node.v2 -->
```json
{
  "abstract_verbatim": "We resolve the Mahler conjecture for general convex bodies positively. For every convex body $`K\\subset\\mathbb R^n`$, <i>n</i> ≥ 1, with Santaló point $`s(K)`$, $`|K|\\,|(K-s(K))^\\circ|\\ge (n+1)^{n+1}/(n!)^2`$, with equality exactly for simplices.",
```

<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We resolve the Mahler conjecture for general convex bodies positively. For every convex body $`K\subset\mathbb R^n`$, <i>n</i> ≥ 1, with Santaló point $`s(K)`$, $`|K|\,|(K-s(K))^\circ|\ge (n+1)^{n+1}/(n!)^2`$, with equality exactly for simplices.

- **claim-mahler-simplex** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-mahler)): For every integer $n\ge1$ and every convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $$P(K)\ge\frac{(n+1)^{n+1}}{(n!)^2}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $K$ is a simplex.
  - Explanation: The volume product of every convex body is bounded below by the simplex constant, and simplices are exactly the minimisers. No symmetry or boundary regularity is assumed.
- **claim-functional-mahler** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-functional-mahler)): For every integer $n\ge1$ and every proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\varphi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$ such that $0<\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi(x)}\,dx<\infty$, $$\left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi(x)}\,dx\right) \left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi^*(y)}\,dy\right)\ge e^n.$$ The second integral is allowed to be infinite, in which case the inequality is immediate.
  - Explanation: For a proper lower-semicontinuous convex potential with finite positive mass, the product of the integrals of the density and of its Fenchel-Legendre dual density is at least the stated exponential constant. It follows from the geometric theorem in all dimensions.
- **claim-cone-reduction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-cone-reduction)): For $U=(0,m)$ and $V=(0,1)$, $$\chi_C(V)\chi_D(U)=\frac{(n!)^2}{m^m}\|K\|\,\|(K-z_0)^\circ\|.$$ For any closed convex solid pointed cone and any interior pair $U\in C$, $V\in C^*$, the two Laplace integrals are finite. Their product and the inner product $\langle U,V\rangle$ are preserved by $$(C,D,U,V)\longmapsto(BC,B^{-\mathsf T}D,BU,B^{-\mathsf T}V)$$ for every invertible linear map $B$.
  - Explanation: The volume product equals, up to an explicit factor, the product of Laplace integrals over a lifted cone and its positive dual. Finiteness and invariance under linear changes of coordinates hold for any solid pointed cone.
- **claim-profile-identities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-profile-identities)): The functions just defined satisfy $$(\mathcal N+2)g=d,\qquad d'=2xd-v'-2g',\qquad \mathcal N d-v''=-(\mathcal N+2)(d+g''). \tag{2}\label{eq:2}$$
  - Explanation: The auxiliary scalar profiles satisfy three differential identities linking them through the Gaussian number operator.
- **claim-scalar-input** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-scalar-input)): The profiles and exact parameters above have the following properties.* *$b-r^2-d>0,\ \|v'-x\|\le .319,\ \mathsf C\le-.341,\ S_q>0$, and $$\lambda t_-^2+t_-\mathsf C+\mathsf K>0,\qquad \lambda t_+^2+t_+\mathsf C+\mathsf K<0,\qquad 2(-.37)\mathsf C-(.37)^2-4\lambda\mathsf K>m_*^2 .$$ Write $$\begin{aligned} b_m&=\frac{(1+t_+)(b-3\eta)}{3(3(b+\eta)-4c)},\qquad e_0=.256,\qquad \alpha=2.26,\qquad h_s=.5,\\ H(z,x)&=4c+4kq(x)+2(k-q(x))S_q(z)-2S_\pi(z)\cdot\pi(x),\qquad H_0(z,x)=H(z,x)+v''(x)-2\kappa . \end{aligned}$$ Pointwise, $$\begin{aligned} H+t_c H_0-\tfrac12t_r(h_s+H_0^2/h_s) > 2(b+\eta+(b+\eta-\kappa)t_-)+2 b_m S_q(z)S_q(x)+2e_0(2t_r)^2+H_0^2/\alpha,\\ 4c+2k(S_q(z)+S_q(x))-S_q(z)S_q(x)-S_\pi(z)\cdot S_\pi(x) >0 . \end{aligned} \tag{3}\label{eq:3}$$ For endpoints $x_-<x_+$, use subscripts $\sigma\in\{-,+\}$ for evaluations, with $-\sigma$ the opposite endpoint. Then $$\begin{aligned} e_K+\Gamma&+ \frac{1}{8\lambda}\sum_\sigma (\overline{\mathsf C}-\mathsf C_{-\sigma}-\overline{\|u-u_\sigma\|^2})^2 +\frac{t_+}{2}\sum_\sigma\|\bar u-u_\sigma\|^2 <\overline{\|u\|^2}-\|\bar u\|^2,\\ e_K&=\overline{\mathsf K}-(\mathsf K_++\mathsf K_-)/2,\\ \Gamma&=\frac{.80}{m_*^2} \big[1.25(\mathsf K_+-\mathsf K_-+t_c(\mathsf C_+-\mathsf C_-))^2+ 5 t_r^2(\mathsf C_+-\mathsf C_-)^2\big]. \end{aligned} \tag{4}\label{eq:4}$$ For coincident endpoints the two sides in the inequality are equal.
  - Explanation: The fixed profiles and parameters satisfy a collected list of scalar inequalities: positivity of the radicand, a deviation bound for the derivative of v, sign conditions on the quadratic profile, a pointwise two-variable layer inequality and a strict segment inequality.
- **claim-biased-projections** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-biased-projections)): For every $z\in\mathbb R$ there is a unique vector $\xi_z$ such that $$X_z=\Pi_C(Z+\xi_z),\qquad Y_z=\Pi_D(-Z-\xi_z),\qquad \mathbb EX_z=a(z)U.\tag{5}\label{eq:5}$$ The map $z\mapsto\xi_z$ is smooth. The a.e. derivative $P_z=D\Pi_C(Z+\xi_z)$ is a symmetric positive contraction, and $$X_z-Y_z=Z+\xi_z,\qquad X_z\cdot Y_z=0,\qquad (\mathbb EP_z)\xi_z'=p(z)U.$$ Define $$B(z)=\frac1m U\cdot\mathbb EY_z,\quad r_1(z)=-B'(z),\quad h(z)=\tau_\Sigma P_z,\quad s_0=\tau\Sigma.$$ Then $$\begin{gathered} h(z)=\frac1m\mathbb E(X_z\cdot Z) =\frac1m\mathbb E\|X_z-\mathbb EX_z\|^2+a(z)B(z),\\ h'=pB+ar_1,\qquad r_1>0. \end{gathered}\tag{6}\label{eq:6}$$
  - Explanation: For each layer parameter there is a unique bias vector making the mean of the projection of the shifted Gaussian lie along a prescribed interior ray. The bias is smooth in the layer, and the projection derivative is a symmetric positive contraction.
- **claim-layer-tails** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-projection-tails)): For every finite $r\ge1$, there are $c_r,C_r>0$ such that $$\begin{aligned} \\|P_z\\|_{L^r}+\\|X_z\\|_{L^r}&\le C_re^{-c_rz^2}&& (z\le0),\\ \\|I-P_z\\|_{L^r}+\\|Y_z\\|_{L^r}&\le C_re^{-c_rz^2}&& (z\ge0). \end{aligned}$$ Here any fixed finite-dimensional matrix norm can be used. Also $B(z)\le C(1+\|z\|)$ for $z\le0$. The positive measure $\mu=h'(z)\,dz$ has total mass $s_0$ and all polynomial moments. The measures with densities $ar_1$ and $pr_1$ have all polynomial moments as well.
  - Explanation: The projection fields and their derivatives have Gaussian decay in the extreme layers in every finite moment, and the layer measures have all polynomial moments.
- **claim-gaussian-sobolev** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-projection-sobolev)): For every smooth $f$ with polynomially bounded derivatives, the centered vector field $$W_f=\int_{\mathbb R}(p(z)Z-X_z+\mathbb EX_z)f'(z)\,dz$$ converges in Gaussian $W^{1,2}$, and its weak Jacobian in $G$ is $D_GW_f=H_f\Sigma^{1/2}$.
  - Explanation: The integrated centered projection field converges in the Gaussian Sobolev space, and its weak Jacobian is the layer-integrated derivative field times the covariance square root.
- **claim-normalization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-normalization)): A linear transformation of the cone data and a symmetric $T$ in the prescribed spectral box can be chosen so that $$\begin{gathered} \mathbb EA=0,\qquad \lambda T^2+\mathbf C\circ T+\mathbf K=0,\qquad \mathbf H:=-\mathbf C-2\lambda T\ge m_*I,\\ \mathbf C=\mathbb E\mathsf C(L),\qquad \mathbf K=\mathbb E\mathsf K(L). \end{gathered}\tag{8}\label{eq:8}$$ Matrix functions here and below use spectral calculus for symmetric matrices.
  - Explanation: Coordinates and the covariance perturbation can be chosen simultaneously so that the mean of the layer field vanishes and a matrix quadratic equation holds with a spectral gap. The proof uses a continuous self-map and a fixed point argument.
- **claim-log-jacobian** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-log-jacobian)): The matrices $\mathcal J_X,\mathcal J_Y$ are positive definite almost surely, their log determinants are integrable, and $$\tau(\log\mathcal J_X+\log\mathcal J_Y) \ge-\gamma v-\tau H_v+\tfrac18N. \tag{9}\label{eq:11}$$
  - Explanation: The two Jacobian matrices built from the projection layers are almost surely positive definite with integrable log determinants, and their log-determinant trace is bounded below with a positive non-nesting surplus.
- **claim-cone-entropy-maps** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-cone-entropy-maps)): With $C_Y=\int W_YB=\int c_Yr_1$, $$\frac1m\log\bigl(\chi_C(V)\chi_D(U)\bigr) \ge\log(2\pi e)+\tau\log\Sigma +\tau(\log\mathcal J_X+\log\mathcal J_Y)-1-C_Y.$$
  - Explanation: Smoothed cone-valued maps of a Gaussian and Jensen's inequality bound the normalised logarithm of the Laplace product from below by the expected log Jacobians and explicit scalar costs.
- **claim-entropy-inequality** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-entropy)): For the cone data and the fixed projection field constructed in Section <a href="#sec:02-projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:02-projections">3</a>, $$\frac1m\log\bigl(\chi_C(V)\chi_D(U)\bigr) \ge \tau(\log\Sigma-T)+\tau_T H_v-\delta[d]+\tfrac18N. \tag{11}\label{eq:10}$$
  - Explanation: The normalised logarithm of the Laplace product is bounded below by a covariance term, a weighted scalar term, a functional defect and the non-nesting surplus.
- **claim-gaussian-covariance** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-gaussian-covariance)): Let $\mathcal X,\mathcal Y$ be centered elements of the Gaussian Sobolev space $W^{1,2}(\mathbb R^m;\mathbb R^m)$. Then $$\mathbb E(\mathcal X\cdot\mathcal Y) =\mathbb E\sum_{i=1}^m (\partial_{G_i}\mathcal X)\cdot \mathcal K_G(\partial_{G_i}\mathcal Y).$$ Moreover, for every square-integrable symmetric matrix field $E$, $\mathcal K_GE$ belongs to the form domain of $1+\mathcal N_G$, for the deterministic weight $\Sigma$.
  - Explanation: The Gaussian covariance of centered Sobolev vector fields is expressed through weak derivatives and the inverse of one plus the number operator, with form regularity under a deterministic matrix weight.
- **claim-layer-covariance** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-layer-covariance)): For smooth scalar tests $f,j$ with $\gamma f=\gamma j=0$, $$\tag{13}\label{eq:13} \langle H_f,\mathcal K_G H_j\rangle_\Sigma =\mathcal Q[f,j]+\operatorname{Ct}(f,j).$$
  - Explanation: The weighted pairing of resolvents of layer integrals equals a scalar bilinear kernel plus a contact term between projections at different layers.
- **claim-kernel-variation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-kernel-variation)): Let $h_f^*,h_j^*$ be smooth scalar tests, and set $f=(1+\mathcal N)h_f^*$, $j=(1+\mathcal N)h_j^*$. Then $$\tag{14}\label{eq:14} \begin{aligned} \mathcal Q_0[f,j]&=\gamma[f h_j^*],\\ (\mathcal Q-s_0\mathcal Q_0)[f,j] &=\Delta_\mu w+\delta[(h_f^*)'(h_j^*)'],& w'&=f'h_j^*+j'h_f^* . \end{aligned}$$ The choice of the additive constant in $w$ is immaterial.
  - Explanation: The reference kernel is inverted explicitly, and the variation of the actual kernel against the reference is expressed through the functional delta evaluated on the product of derivatives of the inverse tests.
- **claim-threshold-defect** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-threshold-defect)): For every such $B_*,f,\Lambda$, $$\tag{15}\label{eq:15} \langle M_f^{B_*},M_f^{B_*}\rangle_\Lambda =2e_\Lambda^{B_*}(f,f)-\operatorname{Nt}_\Lambda(f,f).$$
  - Explanation: The quadratic norm of the difference between the projection layer and a spectral threshold equals twice the threshold defect pairing minus the weighted non-nesting form, with no commutation assumed.
- **claim-layer-spectral** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-layer-spectral)): For smooth scalar tests $f,j$, with $\gamma f=0$ in the last identity, $$\tag{16}\label{eq:16} \begin{aligned} \tau_\Lambda H_j &=\Delta_\Lambda j+\langle R,j'(L)\rangle_\Lambda +e_\Lambda(\iota,j'),\\ \tau_\Sigma H_j&=\Delta_\mu j,\\ \langle H_f,j(L)\rangle_\Sigma &=\mu[fj]+(\Delta_\Sigma-\Delta_\mu)w-e_\Sigma(f,j), \qquad w'=fj'. \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Three identities compare layer integrals with spectral evaluation at the linear Gaussian matrix field.
- **claim-profile-quadratics** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-profile-quadratics)): For every smooth scalar test $l$, $$\tag{17}\label{eq:17} \begin{aligned} \delta[l] &=\Delta_\Sigma\psi_l+\sigma_\Sigma(l) -2e_\Sigma(\iota,h_l)-e_\Sigma(f_l,\iota) -2\operatorname{Ct}(\iota,f_l),\\ D_\Sigma(l)^2+J_\Sigma(l) &=\delta[l^2]-\Delta_\Sigma\psi_{l^2} +2e_\Sigma(f_l,h_l)+\operatorname{Ct}(f_l,f_l). \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: A linear and a quadratic identity separate the nonnegative resolvent form norm from terms depending only on the linear field.
- **claim-quadratic-decomposition** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-quadratic-decomposition)): With the fixed profiles $F=d+\|u\|^2=c+2kq$, one has $$\tag{18}\label{eq:18} \begin{aligned} \delta[d]-\tau_T H_v \leq{}&\Delta_{\rm p}+\sigma_\Sigma(F) -D_\Sigma(u)^2-J_\Sigma(u)-\langle R,v'(L)\rangle_T\\ &-\mathcal L_I[H]-\mathcal L_T[H+v''(x)],\\ \Delta_{\rm p} ={}&\Delta_I\psi_d+\Delta_T(\psi_d-v). \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: For the fixed profiles the signed entropy term is bounded above by linear-field functionals, resolvent form norms and two layer integrals.
- **claim-layers-gaussian** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-layers-gaussian)): With the notation of Section <a href="#sec:04-quadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:04-quadratic">5</a>, $$\sigma_\Sigma(F)-D_\Sigma(u)^2 \le (b+\eta)\tau_\Sigma R^2-\eta\tau_\Sigma R_o^2 +b_m\\|M_{f_q}^L\\|_2^2. \tag{19}\label{eq:19}$$ Moreover, $$\\|M_{f_q}^L\\|_2^2 \le 2\mathcal L_I[S_q(z)S_q(x)].$$
  - Explanation: The Gaussian remainder is controlled by separating odd and even Hermite degrees, with the layer norm of the profile remainder bounded by a layer integral.
- **claim-layers-diagonal** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-layers-diagonal)): Let $B_*$ be a real symmetric matrix, let $0\le P\le I$, and fix $z$ outside the spectrum of $B_*$. In an orthonormal eigenbasis of $B_*$, write $$E=P-1_{(-\infty,z]}(B_*),\qquad a_i=\|x_i-z\|,\qquad \epsilon_i=\operatorname{sgn}(x_i-z).$$ Then $$(E^2)_{ii}\le\epsilon_iE_{ii}.$$ The matrix $S$ with entries $$S_{ij}= \begin{cases} \epsilon_i\sqrt{a_i a_j}\,E_{ij},&\epsilon_i=\epsilon_j,\\ 0,&\epsilon_i\ne\epsilon_j \end{cases}$$ is positive semidefinite, and $\operatorname{tr}S=\sum_i a_i\epsilon_iE_{ii}$. For any nonnegative numbers $w_i$, $$\sum_{i,j}\frac{a_iw_i+a_jw_j}{2}\|E_{ij}\|^2 \le\sum_i a_iw_i\epsilon_iE_{ii}.$$
  - Explanation: A positive-block structure and weighted diagonal bounds hold for the defect between a positive contraction and a spectral threshold, with no commutation assumption.
- **claim-layers-constant** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-layers-constant)): The weighted non-nesting term satisfies $$N_T=2e_T^A(\iota,\iota) \ge t_-N-\sqrt{c_gN/2}\,\\|[A,T]\\|_2, \qquad c_g=2\log2-\frac13. \tag{20}\label{eq:20}$$
  - Explanation: The weighted non-nesting term is bounded below by the unweighted one times the lower spectral endpoint minus a commutator term.
- **claim-layers-variable** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-layers-variable)): The last two terms of Equation <a href="#eq:21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:21">[eq:21]</a> satisfy $$-\mathcal L_I[H-P_0]-\mathcal L_T[H_0] \le\frac{\alpha(\log2+.25)}4\,\\|[L,T]\\|_2^2 -\int(W-W_0)\,d\beta. \tag{22}\label{eq:22}$$
  - Explanation: The variable layer terms are bounded by a commutator norm minus a nonnegative layer integral.
- **claim-linear-spectral-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-linear-spectral-identity)): Let $F_*$ be a smooth scalar function whose derivatives have polynomial growth, and put $f=F_*''$. Then $$\mathbb E[(\mathcal N F_*)(L)] =(\mathbf S-I)\circ\mathbb E f(L)+\mathfrak R(f). \tag{24}\label{eq:24}$$
  - Explanation: The expectation of the number operator applied to a scalar profile at the linear Gaussian matrix field splits into a term involving the quadratic coefficient matrix and a spectral remainder.
- **claim-linear-field-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-linear-field-bound)): For the normalized field satisfying <a href="#eq:8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:8">[eq:8]</a>, with $t_-I\le T\le t_+I$, and the fixed profiles satisfying <a href="#eq:2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:2">[eq:2]</a>–<a href="#eq:4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:4">[eq:4]</a> and $\mathsf C\le-.341$, $$\Delta_{\rm p}-J_\Sigma(u)+.80\\|[L,T]\\|_2^2 \le -1.94\tau T^2.$$ Consequently, with the notation of Equation <a href="#eq:23" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:23">[eq:23]</a>, $$\mathcal E\le -.14\tau T^2-\int(W-W_0)\,d\beta\le0.$$ If $\mathcal E=0$, then $T=0$, the measure $\nu$ is supported on the diagonal, and $\beta=0$.
  - Explanation: For the normalised linear field the combined terms are bounded above by a negative multiple of the covariance perturbation, so the entropy defect is nonpositive. Equality forces the perturbation to vanish, the spectral measure to sit on the diagonal and the layer measure to vanish.
- **claim-entropy-transport** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-entropy-transport)): For every integer $n\ge1$, let $\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\partial\operatorname{supp}\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\nu_i=(\nabla V_i)_\#\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\eta_i)=-\int V_i\,d\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\eta_1)+H(\eta_2)\le -3n+\mathcal T(\nu_1,\nu_2).$$
  - Explanation: For essentially continuous full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, the sum of their entropies is bounded by a convex-conjugate transport cost between their moment measures plus an additive dimensional constant.
- **claim-scalar-ranges** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-ranges)): For every real $x$, one has $\ell>0$. Bounds on values, and absolute bounds on derivatives, are as follows (a dash means no bound asserted): $$\begin{array}{c\|cc\|ccc} &\inf\ \ge &\sup\ \le & \|\dot F\|& \|\ddot F\| & \|n_F\| \\ \hline \mathsf K&-.007&.0275&.066&.238&-\\ \mathsf C&-.501&-.341&.247&.79&.79\\ q&.0775&.2559&.193&.53&.53 \end{array}$$ Also $\|v'(x)-x\|\le .319$, and $$\lambda t_-^2+t_-\mathsf C+\mathsf K>0,\qquad \lambda t_+^2+t_+\mathsf C+\mathsf K<0,\qquad 2(-.37)\mathsf C-(.37)^2-4\lambda\mathsf K > m_*^2.$$ We have $$U_*:=1.507\max(0,-n_{\mathsf K})+ \frac{12\cdot .80}{m_*^2} \left[1.25(\dot{\mathsf K}+t_c\dot{\mathsf C})^2+5t_r^2 \dot{\mathsf C}^2\right] \le .54\,r^2 w^2 .$$ The following additional bounds hold on $\|t\|\ge1.4$: $$\begin{aligned} -.0001\le\mathsf K\le.0191,\quad -.501\le\mathsf C\le-.495,\quad .235\le q,\qquad \|\dot{\mathsf K}\|\le .0211,\quad \|\dot{\mathsf C}\|\le .0084,\quad n_{\mathsf K}\ge0. \end{aligned}$$ For $\|t\|\ge3.6$, $0\le\mathsf K\le .0008$, $\|\mathsf C+.5\|\le .0002$, $q\ge .255$. For $\|x\|\ge5$, $q\ge.2203$. Also $\int_0^\infty (\mathsf K)_+(x)\,dx\le.422$ and $\int_0^{s\sinh(1.4)}(\mathsf K)_+\le .126$.
  - Explanation: Real-line value and derivative bounds for the quadratic profile, the logarithmic-derivative deviation, the covariance inequalities and the integral of the positive part of one profile, together with positivity of the radicand.
- **claim-scalar-layer** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-layer)): We have $.113\le S_q\le .514,\ \rho\le1.104$. For any $z,x$, put $$H=4c+4kq(x)+2(k-q(x))S_q(z)-2S_\pi(z)\cdot\pi(x), \qquad J=H+v''(x)-2\kappa, \quad b_m=\frac{(1+t_+)(b-3\eta)}{3(3(b+\eta)-4c)}.$$ Thus $J$ is the profile $H_0$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:scalar-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:scalar-input">5</a>. With $h_s=.5,\ e_0=.256,\ \alpha=2.26$, $$H+t_cJ-\tfrac12 t_r(h_s+J^2/h_s)-J^2/\alpha > 2(b+\eta+(b+\eta-\kappa)t_-)+2b_m S_q(z)S_q(x)+2e_0(2t_r)^2 .$$ Also $b+\eta+\kappa t_-+.125>0$, $1.053\kappa^2/[8(b+\eta+\kappa t_-+.125)]<e_0$ and $e_0+(\log2+.25)\alpha/4<.80$. We also have $4c+2k(S_q(x)+S_q(z))-S_q(x)S_q(z)-S_\pi(x)\cdot S_\pi(z)>.25$.
  - Explanation: Pointwise bounds on the layer profiles and a strict two-variable inequality hold for every pair of real arguments.
- **claim-scalar-certificates** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-certificates)): The following sample bounds hold with exact rational endpoints. $$\begin{array}{c\|c\|c\|c} &\text{value} & \|\Delta^2\| &\|\Delta\|\\ \hline d&[.3862,.5]&.000260&-\\ \dot d&\pm.1338&.00092&.01037\\ \ddot d&\pm.415&.00421&.03670\\ \mathsf K&[-.00692,.02737]&.000148&-\\ \dot{\mathsf K}&\pm.06542&.000673&-\\ \ddot{\mathsf K}&\pm.2355&.00373&-\\ n_{\mathsf K}&\pm.2355&.00402&-\\ \mathsf C&[-.5,-.3413]&.00049&-\\ \dot{\mathsf C}&\pm.2456&.00222&-\\ \ddot{\mathsf C}&\pm.780&.0129&-\\ n_{\mathsf C}&\pm.780&.0138&-\\ R_v&\pm.318&.00167&-\\ V_2&\pm1.06&.00186&-\\ q&[.0777,.25525]&-&-\\ \dot q&\pm.1908&-&.0127\\ \ddot q&\pm.508&-&.047\\ n_q&\pm.508&-&- \end{array}$$ The same nodes give the following simultaneous bounds: $$\begin{array}{c\|cccc} i&\sup U_*&\inf(\lambda t_-^2+\mathsf Ct_-+\mathsf K)&\sup(\lambda t_+^2+\mathsf Ct_++\mathsf K)& \inf(2(-.37)\mathsf C-.37^2-4\lambda\mathsf K)\\\hline 0{:}151&.537&.00090&-.00074&.132 \end{array}$$ Here the entries for extrema are bounds in the indicated directions. For $56\le i\le144$, sample bounds are $0\le\mathsf K\le.019,\ \|\dot{\mathsf K}\|\le.0207,\ n_{\mathsf K}\ge.00315,\ -.5\le\mathsf C\le-.49512,\ \|\dot{\mathsf C}\|\le.0071,\ q\ge.2353$. For $45\le i\le144$, $q\ge.2204$.* *For $i=56,144$, respectively, the finite sums satisfy $$x_i ((\mathsf K_i)_+ + .238/12800) - 40\sum_{j=1}^{i} (a_{t_j}-a_{t_{j-1}})((\mathsf K_j)_+-(\mathsf K_{j-1})_+) \le .126,\ .335 .$$
  - Explanation: Interval arithmetic at mesh nodes gives rational enclosures of values, differences and simultaneous bounds, together with finite sums bounding the positive-part integrals.
- **claim-scalar-strip** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-strip)): As functions of $t$, the profiles $d,\mathsf K,\mathsf C,R_v,V_2$ are analytic in a neighborhood of $\{t:\|\Im t\|\le .45\}$. On this strip, the first three have modulus at most $1300$, and the last two at most $11000$.
  - Explanation: The profiles are analytic in a common strip around the real axis with explicit modulus bounds, which feed Cauchy estimates.
- **claim-scalar-interpolation** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-interpolation)): Put $\mathcal R(M)=.536M+.00001$. For any of $$F,\dot F,\ddot F,n_F\quad(F=d,\mathsf K,\mathsf C), \qquad R_v,V_2,$$ the error from its central chord, the affine interpolant through the two endpoints of a mesh cell in $[0,3.6]$, is at most $\mathcal R(M)$, where $M$ bounds its absolute second differences whose three nodes lie in the sixteen-node stencil for that cell.
  - Explanation: The error of the central chord on each mesh cell is bounded by an affine function of the local second-difference bound, via a many-node interpolation argument.
- **claim-scalar-tail** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-tail)): For $x\ge64$, set $D=x\partial_x$, $\xi=x^{-2}$, and $L=\log x+\frac12\log(2\pi)-\frac12$. For each row function $F$, the entries are upper bounds for $\|D^jF\|/\xi$, uniformly on this range: $$\begin{array}{c\|ccc} &j=0&j=1&j=2\\\hline \mathsf K-\xi(L-\tfrac32)&.0064&.027&.11\\ d-\tfrac12&.51&1.02&2.1\\ \mathsf C+\tfrac12&.55&1.16&2.61 \end{array}$$ These estimates supply the tail part of the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-ranges" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-ranges">27</a>. On $x\ge64$ we also have $q\in[.255,.2556]$, $S_q\in[.509,.512]$, $V_2\in[.999,1.001]$.
  - Explanation: On the far tail the profiles are controlled by explicit asymptotic remainders with derivative bounds in the dilation operator.
- **claim-segment-estimate** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-segment-estimate)): Put $u=(\pi,q)$. For every pair $x_-<x_+$, define $e_K$ and $\Gamma$ as below. Then $$\begin{aligned} e_K&+\Gamma+ \frac{\sum_{\sigma\in\{-,+\}}(\overline{\mathsf C}-\mathsf C_{-\sigma} -\overline{\|u-u_\sigma\|^2})^2}{8\lambda} +t_+\,\frac12\sum_{\sigma\in\{-,+\}}\|\overline u-u_\sigma\|^2 < \overline{\|u\|^2}-\|\overline u\|^2,\\ e_K&=\overline{\mathsf K}-(\mathsf K_++\mathsf K_-)/2,\\ \Gamma&=\frac{.80}{m_*^2} \left(1.25(\mathsf K_+-\mathsf K_-+t_c(\mathsf C_+-\mathsf C_-))^2+ 5t_r^2(\mathsf C_+-\mathsf C_-)^2\right), \end{aligned}$$ where $-\sigma$ denotes the opposite endpoint. At coincident endpoints, both sides of the asserted inequality are zero.
  - Explanation: For every pair of distinct endpoints a strict inequality holds bounding the chord error, a commutator-type penalty and endpoint deviations by the variance of the vector profile over the segment.
- **claim-segment-chord** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-segment-chord)): For every twice continuously differentiable profile $F$ on the segment, let $t=m+h\beta$ and $$\theta=\frac{\sinh t-\sinh(m-h)}{2\cosh m\sinh h},\qquad S=\frac{\sinh h}{h},\qquad g_h=\frac34S(1+\cosh h).$$ Then $$\overline F-(F_-+F_+)/2 =-\frac{h^2}{3}\int_{-1}^1 W(\beta)\,n_F(m+h\beta)\,\frac{d\beta}2,\qquad W=6\theta(1-\theta)\frac{\cosh m}{\cosh t}\frac{\sinh h}{h},$$ where $n_F$ is evaluated at $x=s\sinh(m+h\beta)$. Moreover, $$0\le W\le\frac32S,\qquad W\le g_h(1-\beta^2),\qquad \int_{-1}^1W\,\frac{d\beta}{2}\le\frac S2(1+\cosh h).$$
  - Explanation: The gap between the average of a smooth profile and the mean of its endpoint values is an integral of its intrinsic second derivative against a nonnegative kernel with explicit bounds.
- **claim-segment-auxiliary** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-segment-auxiliary)): The following bounds hold in the bounded region just specified. For a set in $[0,3.6]$ of length $2H$, $0<H\le T_0$, the mean of $(A_j)_+$ on the set is at most $\alpha_j-\beta_j H$. Also $\dot{\mathsf D}\ge-.022$ on $[0,3.6]$, and for $0\le t\le t'\le3.6$, $$\mathsf C(t')-\mathsf C(t)\le .000124,\qquad q(t)-q(t')\le .000084 .$$ We will use $b_a\le .0163$, and primitive bounds $$\begin{array}{ll} l<0: & e_K+\Gamma\le \min(.018,.0146\cdot4 h^2+.00010)+.00015,\\ l\ge0: & e_K+\Gamma\le .01815+1/(312\sinh^2(2h)). \end{array} \tag{s1}\label{eq:s1}$$
  - Explanation: Concentration, almost-monotonicity and primitive bounds hold in the bounded region and control the chord and endpoint costs.

Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n e_K+\\Gamma&+\n \\frac{1}{8\\lambda}\\sum_\\sigma\n (\\overline{\\mathsf C}-\\mathsf C_{-\\sigma}-\\overline{|u-u_\\sigma|^2})^2\n +\\frac{t_+}{2}\\sum_\\sigma|\\bar u-u_\\sigma|^2\n <\\overline{|u|^2}-|\\bar u|^2,\\\\\n e_K&=\\overline{\\mathsf K}-(\\mathsf K_++\\mathsf K_-)/2,\\\\\n \\Gamma&=\\frac{.80}{m_*^2}\n \\big[1.25(\\mathsf K_+-\\mathsf K_-+t_c(\\mathsf C_+-\\mathsf C_-))^2+\n           5 t_r^2(\\mathsf C_+-\\mathsf C_-)^2\\big].\n\\end{aligned}                                                       \\tag{4}\\label{eq:4}$$ For coincident endpoints the two sides in the inequality are equal.",
```json
      "explanation": "The fixed profiles and parameters satisfy a collected list of scalar inequalities: positivity of the radicand, a deviation bound for the derivative of v, sign conditions on the quadratic profile, a pointwise two-variable layer inequality and a strict segment inequality.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-biased-projections",
        "exact_quote": "For every $z\\in\\mathbb R$ there is a unique vector $\\xi_z$ such that $$X_z=\\Pi_C(Z+\\xi_z),\\qquad Y_z=\\Pi_D(-Z-\\xi_z),\\qquad\n\\mathbb EX_z=a(z)U.\\tag{5}\\label{eq:5}$$ The map $z\\mapsto\\xi_z$ is smooth. The a.e. derivative $P_z=D\\Pi_C(Z+\\xi_z)$ is a symmetric positive contraction, and $$X_z-Y_z=Z+\\xi_z,\\qquad X_z\\cdot Y_z=0,\\qquad\n(\\mathbb EP_z)\\xi_z'=p(z)U.$$ Define $$B(z)=\\frac1m U\\cdot\\mathbb EY_z,\\quad r_1(z)=-B'(z),\\quad\nh(z)=\\tau_\\Sigma P_z,\\quad s_0=\\tau\\Sigma.$$ Then $$\\begin{gathered}\nh(z)=\\frac1m\\mathbb E(X_z\\cdot Z)\n=\\frac1m\\mathbb E|X_z-\\mathbb EX_z|^2+a(z)B(z),\\\\\nh'=pB+ar_1,\\qquad r_1>0.\n\\end{gathered}\\tag{6}\\label{eq:6}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-087.2"
      },

        "exact_quote": "Let $h_f^*,h_j^*$ be smooth scalar tests, and set $f=(1+\\mathcal N)h_f^*$, $j=(1+\\mathcal N)h_j^*$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-layers-diagonal",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B_*$ be a real symmetric matrix, let $0\\le P\\le I$, and fix $z$ outside the spectrum of $B_*$. For any nonnegative numbers $w_i$, $$\\sum_{i,j}\\frac{a_iw_i+a_jw_j}{2}|E_{ij}|^2\n \\le\\sum_i a_iw_i\\epsilon_iE_{ii}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-087.2"
      },

        "exact_quote": "For the normalized field satisfying <a href=\"#eq:8\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:8\">[eq:8]</a>, with $t_-I\\le T\\le t_+I$, and the fixed profiles satisfying <a href=\"#eq:2\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:2\">[eq:2]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:4\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:4\">[eq:4]</a> and $\\mathsf C\\le-.341$, $$\\Delta_{\\rm p}-J_\\Sigma(u)+.80\\|[L,T]\\|_2^2\n       \\le -1.94\\tau T^2.$$ Consequently, with the notation of Equation <a href=\"#eq:23\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:23\">[eq:23]</a>, $$\\mathcal E\\le -.14\\tau T^2-\\int(W-W_0)\\,d\\beta\\le0.$$ If $\\mathcal E=0$, then $T=0$, the measure $\\nu$ is supported on the diagonal, and $\\beta=0$.",

      "exact_statement": "For the normalized field satisfying <a href=\"#eq:8\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:8\">[eq:8]</a>, with $t_-I\\le T\\le t_+I$, and the fixed profiles satisfying <a href=\"#eq:2\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:2\">[eq:2]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:4\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:4\">[eq:4]</a> and $\\mathsf C\\le-.341$, $$\\Delta_{\\rm p}-J_\\Sigma(u)+.80\\|[L,T]\\|_2^2\n       \\le -1.94\\tau T^2.$$ Consequently, with the notation of Equation <a href=\"#eq:23\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:23\">[eq:23]</a>, $$\\mathcal E\\le -.14\\tau T^2-\\int(W-W_0)\\,d\\beta\\le0.$$ If $\\mathcal E=0$, then $T=0$, the measure $\\nu$ is supported on the diagonal, and $\\beta=0$.",
      "explanation": "For the normalised linear field the combined terms are bounded above by a negative multiple of the covariance perturbation, so the entropy defect is nonpositive.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-entropy-transport",
        "exact_quote": "For every integer $n\\ge1$, let $\\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\\partial\\operatorname{supp}\\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\\nu_i=(\\nabla V_i)_\\#\\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\\eta_i)=-\\int V_i\\,d\\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\\eta_1)+H(\\eta_2)\\le -3n+\\mathcal T(\\nu_1,\\nu_2).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-087.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-cor-entropy-transport",
          "exact_quote": "For every integer $n\\ge1$, let $\\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\\partial\\operatorname{supp}\\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\\nu_i=(\\nabla V_i)_\\#\\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\\eta_i)=-\\int V_i\\,d\\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\\eta_1)+H(\\eta_2)\\le -3n+\\mathcal T(\\nu_1,\\nu_2).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-087.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every integer $n\\ge1$, let $\\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\\partial\\operatorname{supp}\\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\\nu_i=(\\nabla V_i)_\\#\\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\\eta_i)=-\\int V_i\\,d\\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\\eta_1)+H(\\eta_2)\\le -3n+\\mathcal T(\\nu_1,\\nu_2).$$",

        "exact_quote": "For every real $x$, one has $\\ell>0$. Bounds on values, and absolute bounds on derivatives, are as follows (a dash means no bound asserted): $$\\begin{array}{c|cc|ccc}\n &\\inf\\ \\ge &\\sup\\ \\le & |\\dot F|& |\\ddot F| & |n_F| \\\\ \\hline\n \\mathsf K&-.007&.0275&.066&.238&-\\\\\n \\mathsf C&-.501&-.341&.247&.79&.79\\\\\n q&.0775&.2559&.193&.53&.53\n\\end{array}$$ Also $|v'(x)-x|\\le .319$, and $$\\lambda t_-^2+t_-\\mathsf C+\\mathsf K>0,\\qquad\n \\lambda t_+^2+t_+\\mathsf C+\\mathsf K<0,\\qquad\n 2(-.37)\\mathsf C-(.37)^2-4\\lambda\\mathsf K > m_*^2.$$ We have $$U_*:=1.507\\max(0,-n_{\\mathsf K})+\n \\frac{12\\cdot .80}{m_*^2}\n \\left[1.25(\\dot{\\mathsf K}+t_c\\dot{\\mathsf C})^2+5t_r^2 \\dot{\\mathsf C}^2\\right]\n \\le .54\\,r^2 w^2 .$$ The following additional bounds hold on $|t|\\ge1.4$: $$\\begin{aligned}\n -.0001\\le\\mathsf K\\le.0191,\\quad -.501\\le\\mathsf C\\le-.495,\\quad .235\\le q,\\qquad\n |\\dot{\\mathsf K}|\\le .0211,\\quad |\\dot{\\mathsf C}|\\le .0084,\\quad n_{\\mathsf K}\\ge0.\n\\end{aligned}$$ For $|t|\\ge3.6$, $0\\le\\mathsf K\\le .0008$, $|\\mathsf C+.5|\\le .0002$, $q\\ge .255$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-interpolation",
        "exact_quote": "Put $\\mathcal R(M)=.536M+.00001$. For any of $$F,\\dot F,\\ddot F,n_F\\quad(F=d,\\mathsf K,\\mathsf C),\n \\qquad R_v,V_2,$$ the error from its central chord, the affine interpolant through the two endpoints of a mesh cell in $[0,3.6]$, is at most $\\mathcal R(M)$, where $M$ bounds its absolute second differences whose three nodes lie in the sixteen-node stencil for that cell.",
        "source_id": "PM-087.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **convex body** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A *convex body* in $\mathbb R^n$ is a compact convex set with nonempty interior.
- **Santaló point** ([source](#sec:introduction)): The minimum of $\|(K-z)^\circ\|$ is attained at a unique point $s(K)\in\operatorname{int}K$, the *Santaló point* (a proof is included in Section
- **volume product** ([source](#sec:introduction)): The volume product is $$P(K)=\|K\|\,\|(K-s(K))^\circ\| =\inf_{z\in\operatorname{int}K}\|K\|\,\|(K-z)^\circ\|.$$
- **polar body at an interior point** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For $z\in\operatorname{int}K$, define $$(K-z)^\circ =\{y\in\mathbb R^n:\langle y,x-z\rangle\le1\text{ for every }x\in K\}.$$
- **simplex** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A simplex is the convex hull of $n+1$ affinely independent points.
- **support function** ([source](#pm-h-7)): The support function of a convex body $K$ is $h_K(u)=\sup_{x\in K}\langle u,x\rangle$.
- **positive duality of cones** ([source](#pm-h-8)): For cones, the star denotes this *positive* duality.
- **Laplace integrals on dual cones** ([source](#pm-h-8)): For interior vectors $V\in D$ and $U\in C$, write $$\chi_C(V)=\int_C e^{-\langle V,x\rangle}\,dx,\qquad \chi_D(U)=\int_D e^{-\langle U,x\rangle}\,dx$$ using $m$-dimensional Lebesgue measure.
- **Gaussian profile notation** ([source](#pm-h-9)): Write $\phi(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-x^2/2}$, $p(x)=\int_{-\infty}^x\phi(y)\,dy$, and $\gamma f=\int f(x)\phi(x)\,dx$.
- **Gaussian number operator** ([source](#pm-h-9)): Put $\mathcal N=x\partial_x-\partial_x^2$.
- **nearest-point projection and layer mean function** ([source](#pm-h-12)): Let $\Pi_C$ denote Euclidean nearest-point projection onto $C$, and put $a(z)=\phi(z)+zp(z)$, so that $a>0$ and $a'=p$

```json
      "exact_definition": "Let $\\Pi_C$ denote Euclidean nearest-point projection onto $C$, and put $a(z)=\\phi(z)+zp(z)$, so that $a>0$ and $a'=p$",
      "interpretation": "The projection onto the cone and the scalar function prescribing the mean of the biased projection.",
      "kind": "notation",
      "name": "nearest-point projection and layer mean function",
      "scope": "Biased projection field."
    }
  ],

  "pm_id": "PM-087.2",
  "schema": "pm.objects.v2"
}
```

| q-entropy-transport-constant | additive constant in the entropy-transport bound | = | $-3n$ | every dimension |
| q-param-b | profile parameter b | = | $.602$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-param-c | profile parameter c | = | $.365$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-param-r | circle radius r | = | $.22$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-param-w | angular frequency w | = | $4.6$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-param-s | scale s | = | $3.6$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-param-eta | tolerance eta | = | $.022$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-param-kappa | weight kappa | = | $1.16$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-param-lambda | quadratic weight lambda | = | $.65$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-param-aR | profile parameter a_R | = | $7.5$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-spectral-lower | lower spectral endpoint t_- | = | $-.022$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-spectral-upper | upper spectral endpoint t_+ | = | $.064$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-spectral-center | box centre t_c | = | $.021$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-spectral-radius | box radius t_r | = | $.043$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-spectral-gap | spectral gap constant m_* | = | $.352$ | independent of body and dimension |
| q-const-e0 | commutator constant e_0 | = | $.256$ | fixed profiles |
| q-const-alpha | weight alpha | = | $2.26$ | fixed profiles |
| q-const-hs | scale h_s | = | $.5$ | fixed profiles |
| q-deviation-bound | deviation of v' from the identity | ≤ | $.319$ | fixed profiles |
| q-C-upper | upper bound for the profile C | ≤ | $-.341$ | fixed profiles |
| q-covariance-spectrum | spectrum of the Gaussian covariance | ~ | $[.978,1.064]$ | symmetric T in the spectral box |
| q-linear-field-margin | entropy defect upper bound | ≤ | $-.14\tau T^2$ | fixed profiles |
| q-linear-field-coefficient | negative coefficient of the covariance trace term | ≤ | $-1.94\tau T^2$ | fixed profiles |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-cone-reduction** [claim](#stmt-lem-cone-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cone-reduction): Compute the Laplace integrals of the lifted cone and its dual by slicing; transport under linear maps by change of variables. -> Volume product expressed as a product of Laplace integrals
- **step-profile-identities** [claim](#stmt-lem-profile-identities) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-profile-identities): Differentiate the explicit profiles and substitute. -> Three differential identities
- **step-scalar-input** [claim](#stmt-prop-scalar-input) / [proof](#sec:07-scalar): Collect the scalar inequalities proved by the appendix certificates, interpolation and tail arguments. -> Dimension-free scalar input
- **step-biased-projections** [claim](#stmt-lem-biased-projections) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-biased-projections): Minimise a coercive smooth convex functional to fix the bias; use the implicit function theorem and Gaussian integration by parts. -> Smooth biased projection field with symmetric contraction derivative
- **step-layer-tails** [claim](#stmt-lem-projection-tails) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-projection-tails): Use Lipschitz bounds and Gaussian tails, splitting the variance expression by the size of the Gaussian input. -> Gaussian layer decay and moment bounds
- **step-gaussian-sobolev** [claim](#stmt-lem-projection-sobolev) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-projection-sobolev): Truncated layer integrals are Cauchy in the Gaussian Sobolev norm; closedness of the weak derivative. -> Weak Jacobian of the integrated field
- **step-normalization** [claim](#stmt-prop-normalization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-normalization): Continuity, a covariance update by order-preserving square roots, an inward estimate and Brouwer's fixed point theorem. -> Normalised coordinates and covariance
- **step-log-jacobian** [claim](#stmt-lem-log-jacobian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-log-jacobian): Compare the layer log determinant with the Gaussian reference and bound the nonnegative surplus by the non-nesting kernel. -> Lower bound for the log-Jacobian trace
- **step-cone-entropy-maps** [claim](#stmt-lem-cone-entropy-maps) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cone-entropy-maps): Smooth the maps, change variables, apply Jensen's inequality and pass to limits. -> Lower bound for the normalised log Laplace product
- **step-entropy-inequality** [claim](#stmt-prop-entropy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-entropy): Identify the scalar terms by integration by parts and the profile identities. -> Entropy inequality with functional defect
- **step-gaussian-covariance** [claim](#stmt-lem-gaussian-covariance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-covariance): Hermite expansion of the fields and of the resolvent. -> Covariance formula with matrix weight
- **step-layer-covariance** [claim](#stmt-lem-layer-covariance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-layer-covariance): Compute the covariance in two ways, from the Sobolev representation and from the layers. -> Kernel plus contact identity
- **step-kernel-variation** [claim](#stmt-lem-kernel-variation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-kernel-variation): Primitive formulas, symmetry and the product rule for the number operator. -> Kernel variation identity
- **step-threshold-defect** [claim](#stmt-lem-threshold-defect) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-threshold-defect): Expand products of threshold defects over ordered layers. -> Quadratic defect identity
- **step-layer-spectral** [claim](#stmt-lem-layer-spectral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-layer-spectral): Integrate by parts and use the layer representation of the remainder. -> Layer-to-spectral comparison
- **step-profile-quadratics** [claim](#stmt-lem-profile-quadratics) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-profile-quadratics): Evaluate one quantity in two ways and expand the resolvent form norm. -> Linear and quadratic profile identities
- **step-quadratic-decomposition** [claim](#stmt-prop-quadratic-decomposition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-quadratic-decomposition): Apply the linear identity to F, subtract the quadratic identity for each component, discard a nonnegative contact term. -> Decomposition of the entropy term
- **step-layers-gaussian** [claim](#stmt-lem-layers-gaussian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-layers-gaussian): Complete squares on odd Hermite degrees and diagonalise a two-by-two form on even degrees. -> Control of the Gaussian remainder
- **step-layers-diagonal** [claim](#stmt-lem-layers-diagonal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-layers-diagonal): Block compression of positive matrices and diagonal congruence. -> Positive blocks and weighted diagonal bound
- **step-layers-constant** [claim](#stmt-lem-layers-constant) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-layers-constant): Split a scalar spectral kernel into a positive comparison part and a remainder; weighted Cauchy-Schwarz. -> Bound for the weighted constant layer
- **step-layers-variable** [claim](#stmt-lem-layers-variable) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-layers-variable): Matrix-order bound for the weight, scalar integral estimate for the remainder, completion of a square. -> Bound for the variable layer
- **step-linear-spectral-identity** [claim](#stmt-lem-linear-spectral-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-linear-spectral-identity): Divided-difference derivative formula and Gaussian integration by parts. -> Spectral identity for the number operator
- **step-linear-field-bound** [claim](#stmt-prop-linear-field-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-linear-field-bound): Reduce to scalar functionals, bound the weighted Dirichlet and commutator terms by the segment inequality, assemble. -> Nonpositive entropy defect with equality information
- **step-mahler-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-mahler) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-linear-field-bound-2): Combine the entropy and linear-field bounds; equality forces commuting coefficients and a diagonal projection derivative, hence an orthant cone and a simplex; converse by direct volume computation. -> Sharp bound and simplex classification
- **step-functional-mahler** [claim](#stmt-cor-functional-mahler) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-linear-field-bound-3): Apply the all-dimensional geometric-to-functional implication of Fradelizi and Meyer. -> Functional Mahler inequality
- **step-entropy-transport** [claim](#stmt-cor-entropy-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-entropy-transport): Apply Gozlan's equivalence to the functional inequality. -> Entropy-transport inequality
- **step-scalar-ranges** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-ranges) / [proof](#pm-h-50): Chord errors between nodes plus tail bounds give the real-line profile bounds. -> Real profile bounds
- **step-scalar-layer** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-layer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-tail-2): Row-wise enclosures and concavity reduce the pointwise inequality to finitely many vertex evaluations. -> Pointwise layer inequalities
- **step-scalar-certificates** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-certificates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-certificates): Interval arithmetic at mesh nodes with explicit Taylor and continued-fraction enclosures. -> Rational certificates
- **step-scalar-strip** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-strip) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-strip): Bound the profiles in a complex strip using Laplace integrals and nonvanishing estimates. -> Analyticity and modulus bounds
- **step-scalar-interpolation** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-interpolation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-interpolation): Cauchy estimates for the analytic remainder and Newton interpolation for the chord comparison. -> Chord error bound
- **step-scalar-tail** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-tail) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-tail): Asymptotic expansions with explicit derivative-vector bookkeeping. -> Tail derivative bounds
- **step-segment-chord** [claim](#stmt-lem-segment-chord) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-segment-chord): Trapezoidal error in the physical coordinate and hyperbolic factorisations. -> Chord kernel representation
- **step-segment-auxiliary** [claim](#stmt-lem-segment-auxiliary) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-segment-auxiliary): Threshold sums for concentration, starred primitive samples and clipped monotonicity. -> Concentration and primitive bounds
- **step-segment-estimate** [claim](#stmt-prop-segment-estimate) / [proof](#pm-h-77): Case analysis by width and location with exact budgets and polynomial certificates. -> Strict segment inequality

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-simplex-volume-product**: For the simplex conv{0,e_1,...,e_n} with the interior point z_0 = 1/m (m=n+1), the polar body is a simplex whose volume, times the volume of K, equals the lower bound constant, so the bound is attained.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-certify-nonsimplex**: Treat the exact simplex computation as evidence about the minimum for arbitrary bodies, or use a numerical near-match of a volume product to certify that a body is a simplex. -> The finite computation covers one explicit body and centre per dimension; it does not prove the lower bound or the equality classification for other bodies.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The converse direction of the main theorem is an explicit finite computation: the polar body of the simplex at the stated centre is a simplex with explicit vertices, whose volume is a determinant. The lower bound itself is not finite and is not tested.

```python
# INPUTS: one integer n >= 2 (dimension) on stdin.
# PRECONDITIONS: n is a positive integer; K = conv{0,e_1,...,e_n}, z_0 = all-ones/(n+1).
# OUTPUTS: the exact volume product |K||(K-z_0)^polar| as a reduced fraction num/den.
# TIME: O(n^3) exact rational operations for the determinant.
# SPACE: O(n^2) rationals.
# ARITHMETIC: exact rationals (fractions.Fraction); no floating point.
# FAILURE: non-integer input raises an error; n < 1 is outside the contract.
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
n = int(sys.stdin.read().split()[0])
m = n + 1
verts = [[Fraction(m)] * n] + [[Fraction(-m) if j == i else Fraction(0) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
rows = [[verts[i + 1][j] - verts[0][j] for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
d = Fraction(1)
for c in range(n):
    p = next(r for r in range(c, n) if rows[r][c] != 0)
    if p != c:
        rows[c], rows[p] = rows[p], rows[c]
        d = -d
    d *= rows[c][c]
    for r in range(c + 1, n):
        f = rows[r][c] / rows[c][c]
        rows[r] = [rows[r][j] - f * rows[c][j] for j in range(n)]
fact = 1
for k in range(2, n + 1): fact *= k
vol_polar = abs(d) / fact
vol_k = Fraction(1, fact)
prod = vol_polar * vol_k
print(f"{prod.numerator}/{prod.denominator}")
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an existence-free inequality with an equality classification. It gives no procedure or interface that a library could implement. A library could only use the stated bound as an assertion. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Convex bodies appear only as abstract compact convex sets in arbitrary dimension. The paper has no simulation model, collision query or time-stepping scheme. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The inequality can serve as a sanity assertion in a geometry test harness. For a body whose volume and polar volume are computed independently, the product must not fall below the stated simplex constant, and equality is a simplex diagnostic. The paper itself supplies no computational method. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The argument rests on Gaussian projections, a fixed-point existence step and rigorous interval certificates for scalar profiles. It is not a finite algorithm with a running-time guarantee. The scalar certificates only verify fixed inequalities. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A simplex is defined only as a convex hull of affinely independent points. The paper proposes no data layout, index or representation scheme. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only numerical element is a set of exact rational certificates for scalar profile bounds. The paper has no kernels, precision model or hardware mapping. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a purely geometric extremal statement. It has no hardness assumption, adversary model or protocol. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The functional corollary bounds a product of integrals of a log-concave density and its conjugate. It is a pure inequality with no learning, estimation or training procedure. The potential must be convex and the integral finite and positive. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The entropy-transport corollary is an inequality between entropies and a convex-conjugate transport cost. It does not give a solver or a convergent scheme. It also requires essentially continuous densities with finite first moments of the moment measures. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no communication, consensus or fault model. The only invariance, under invertible affine maps, is a geometric symmetry and not a system property. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Possible uses lie in pure geometry, such as normalizing bodies by their Santaló point. They are not engineering constructions in the sense required here. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-convex-body-class** (hypothesis): The inequality is stated only for convex bodies, meaning compact convex sets with nonempty interior. Sets that are non-convex, unbounded or have empty interior are outside the theorem. Blocked use: ['Applying the simplex lower bound to a non-convex, unbounded or lower-dimensional set', 'Applying the bound to a point cloud without first taking its convex body']
- **limit-equality-iff-simplex** (formal_scope): Equality is classified only through the stated if and only if for convex bodies. An approximate numerical match of a computed product with the constant does not certify that a body is a simplex. Blocked use: ['Declaring a body a simplex from a floating-point near-equality of the volume product', 'Inferring a minimiser classification for objects that are not convex bodies']
- **limit-functional-integrability** (hypothesis): The functional inequality requires a proper lower-semicontinuous convex potential whose density integral is finite and positive. Potentials outside this class are not covered. Blocked use: ['Applying the exponential lower bound to a nonconvex potential', 'Applying it to a density with zero or infinite total mass']
- **limit-functional-equality-not-transferred** (formal_scope): The simplex equality classification of the geometric theorem does not transfer to the functional inequality. The limiting implication does not carry equality cases. Blocked use: ['Concluding that functional equality forces a simplex indicator potential', 'Reading the sharpness example as a classification of all functional extremisers']
- **limit-entropy-transport-continuity** (hypothesis): The entropy-transport inequality needs essentially continuous densities: the density vanishes at almost every boundary point of its support. This is an additional hypothesis, not a requirement of the geometric theorem. Blocked use: ['Using the entropy bound for log-concave densities that do not vanish on the support boundary', 'Using it when the moment measures lack finite first moments']
- **limit-normalization-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The simultaneous choice of coordinates and covariance comes from a fixed point argument on a compact convex set. It gives existence only and no procedure to compute the normalised data. Blocked use: ['Presenting the normalisation as a computable algorithm', 'Deriving a running-time bound for the proof']
- **limit-scalar-constants-not-optimal** (approximation): The fixed numerical constants are tuned to make finite certificates close, and they are not claimed to be optimal. They hold for these specific profiles and parameters only. Blocked use: ['Reading the constants as sharp thresholds', 'Reusing the certificates after changing any profile parameter']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 087 (`lean/docs/087.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The symmetric Mahler conjecture and its equality cases](../../preprints/The-symmetric-Mahler-conjecture-and-its-equality-cases-September-22-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [The Mahler Conjecture for General Convex Bodies](../../preprints/The-Mahler-Conjecture-for-General-Convex-Bodies-September-22-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Symplectic Balls in Symmetric Polar Products](../../preprints/Symplectic-Balls-in-Symmetric-Polar-Products-September-22-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The symmetric Mahler conjecture predicts $|K||K^\circ|\ge4^n/n!$ for every origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$. The formalization establishes this for every $n\ge1$ and characterizes equality exactly by invertible linear images of Hanner bodies, built from intervals using Cartesian products and convex-hull joins.
>
> The nonsymmetric Mahler conjecture and the functional inequalities are not included.
>
> The general Mahler conjecture gives a sharp lower bound for the volume product of a convex body and its polar. For every $n\ge1$ and compact convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ with nonempty interior, the formalization proves
> $\inf_{z\in\mathrm{int}\,K}|K|\,|(K-z)^\circ|\ge (n+1)^{n+1}/(n!)^2$.
> Equality holds exactly when $K$ is a simplex. The paper's functional inequality is outside this selected statement.
>
> For an origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge2$, the formalized result determines the symplectic ball capacity of $\mathrm{int}\,K\times\mathrm{int}\,K^\circ$: its Gromov width is $4$, and every ball of capacity $0<c<4$ embeds symplectically into it. The normalization assigns capacity $\pi r^2$ to a ball of radius $r$.
>
> No boundary smoothness or strict convexity is assumed. An embedding at capacity exactly $4$ is not asserted.

| declaration | kind |
|---|---|
| `Rn` | abbrev |
| `polarAt` | def |
| `volumeProduct` | def |
| `general_mahler` | theorem |

Comparator source `GeneralMahler.lean` (1238 bytes, sha256 `4c2ff125052477b16e8ba2e85e641e65fc6790e08da7990fa309a5e9033f9364`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open Set MeasureTheory
open scoped RealInnerProductSpace

namespace GeneralMahler

abbrev Rn (n : ℕ) := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)

/-- The polar of a convex body translated by an interior center. -/
def polarAt {n : ℕ} (K : Set (Rn n)) (z : Rn n) : Set (Rn n) :=
  {y | ∀ x ∈ K, ⟪y, x - z⟫ ≤ 1}

/-- The volume product minimized over the interior centers of the body. -/
def volumeProduct {n : ℕ} (K : Set (EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n))) : ℝ :=
  sInf ((fun z => (volume K).toReal * (volume (polarAt K z)).toReal) ''
    interior K)

/-- The sharp general Mahler inequality, with equality exactly for simplices. -/
theorem general_mahler {n : ℕ} (hn : 1 ≤ n)
    (K : Set (EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)))
    (hcompact : IsCompact K) (hconvex : Convex ℝ K)
    (hinterior : (interior K).Nonempty) :
    ((n : ℝ) + 1) ^ (n + 1) / (Nat.factorial n : ℝ) ^ 2 ≤ volumeProduct K ∧
      (volumeProduct K = ((n : ℝ) + 1) ^ (n + 1) / (Nat.factorial n : ℝ) ^ 2 ↔
        ∃ vertices : Fin (n + 1) → EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n),
          AffineIndependent ℝ vertices ∧ K = convexHull ℝ (range vertices)) := by
  sorry

end GeneralMahler

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We resolve the Mahler conjecture for general convex bodies positively. For every convex body $`K\subset\mathbb R^n`$, <i>n</i> ≥ 1, with Santaló point $`s(K)`$, $`|K|\,|(K-s(K))^\circ|\ge (n+1)^{n+1}/(n!)^2`$, with equality exactly for simplices.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The Mahler conjecture asks which convex bodies have the smallest product of their volume and the volume of a suitably centered polar body. We resolve the conjecture positively: simplices are exactly the minimizers in every positive dimension.

A *convex body* in $\mathbb R^n$ is a compact convex set with nonempty interior. We write $|A|$ for the $n$-dimensional Lebesgue volume of a measurable set $A$, and $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ for the Euclidean inner product. For $z\in\operatorname{int}K$, define $$(K-z)^\circ
 =\{y\in\mathbb R^n:\langle y,x-z\rangle\le1\text{ for every }x\in K\}.$$ The minimum of $|(K-z)^\circ|$ is attained at a unique point $s(K)\in\operatorname{int}K$, the *Santaló point* (a proof is included in Section <a href="#sec:01-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:01-cones">2</a>). The volume product is $$P(K)=|K|\,|(K-s(K))^\circ|
     =\inf_{z\in\operatorname{int}K}|K|\,|(K-z)^\circ|.$$ It is invariant under invertible affine maps. A simplex is the convex hull of $n+1$ affinely independent points.

<a id="stmt-thm-mahler"></a>
<div id="thm:mahler" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (General Mahler conjecture). *For every integer $n\ge1$ and every convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $$P(K)\ge\frac{(n+1)^{n+1}}{(n!)^2}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $K$ is a simplex.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mahler">1</a> applies without symmetry or boundary regularity assumptions. It also gives the displayed lower bound for $|K|\,|(K-z)^\circ|$ at every interior translation point $z$. The symmetric Mahler conjecture asks for the different, stronger constant $4^n/n!$ on the restricted class of centrally symmetric bodies; that sharper statement is separate from Theorem <a href="#thm:mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mahler">1</a> and is proved in the companion paper (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[OpenAISymmetricMahler2026](#ref-OpenAISymmetricMahler2026)].

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and context

The lower-volume-product problem grew out of Mahler’s work on polarity and the geometry of numbers. His polygon paper established the general planar inequality and its triangle equality case among polygons (Mahler 1938) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Mahler1938](#ref-Mahler1938)]; his transference paper formulated the higher-dimensional symmetric problem (Mahler 1939) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Mahler1939](#ref-Mahler1939)]. Meyer later gave a new proof of the planar general inequality and completed its equality characterization for arbitrary planar convex bodies: triangles are exactly the minimizers (Meyer 1991) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Meyer1991](#ref-Meyer1991)]. The affine center used here belongs to the classical Santaló theory (Santaló 1949) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Santalo1949](#ref-Santalo1949)]; we give its elementary existence and uniqueness argument in Section <a href="#sec:01-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:01-cones">2</a>.

Several lines of work explain the distinction between an asymptotic lower bound and the exact simplex constant. The inverse Santaló theorem of Bourgain and Milman gives $|K|\,|K^\circ|\ge c^n|B_2^n|^2$ for origin-symmetric bodies, where $c>0$ is absolute and $B_2^n$ is the Euclidean unit ball (Bourgain and Milman 1987) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BourgainMilman1987](#ref-BourgainMilman1987)]. Kuperberg obtained explicit lower bounds through Gauss linking integrals, with separate conclusions in the symmetric and general settings (Kuperberg 2008) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Kuperberg2008](#ref-Kuperberg2008)]. Nazarov developed a complex-analytic proof using Bergman kernels and Hörmander estimates (Nazarov 2012) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Nazarov2012](#ref-Nazarov2012)]; Mastrantonis and Rubinstein extended that direct analytical method to nonsymmetric bodies (Mastrantonis and Rubinstein 2024) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[MastrantonisRubinstein2024](#ref-MastrantonisRubinstein2024)]. These approaches establish exponential-order lower bounds without the sharp constant asserted in Theorem <a href="#thm:mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mahler">1</a>.

Exact results in substantial special classes also preceded the general statement. Meyer and Reisner used shadow systems, one-parameter deformations of convex bodies, to prove the sharp inequality and simplex equality for polytopes with at most $n+3$ vertices. Their key input is convexity of the reciprocal polar volume at the Santaló point along such a deformation (Meyer and Reisner 2006, Theorems 1 and 10) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[MeyerReisner2006](#ref-MeyerReisner2006)]. Kim and Reisner established strict local minimality, with a quantitative estimate, at every simplex (Kim and Reisner 2011, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KimReisner2011](#ref-KimReisner2011)]; this is local information about the space of convex bodies. In dimension three, Iriyeh and Shibata proved the symmetric conjecture (Iriyeh and Shibata 2020) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[IriyehShibata2020](#ref-IriyehShibata2020)]. The 2026 preprint of Chen, Li, Xi, and Xu proves $P(K)\ge64/9$ for every three-dimensional convex body, with equality exactly for tetrahedra (Chen et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[ChenLiXiXu2026](#ref-ChenLiXiXu2026)]. Their shadow flows constrain the permitted deformations so that they can treat arbitrary polytopes in dimension three and then pass to general bodies. In contrast, the argument below works with projections onto a fixed cone and their Gaussian averages. Theorem <a href="#thm:mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mahler">1</a> concerns arbitrary convex bodies in all dimensions, including its global equality classification.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The first step is the cone/Laplace correspondence developed by Klartag (Klartag 2018, Lemma 2.1 and Corollary 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Klartag2018](#ref-Klartag2018)]. Choose $z_0\in\operatorname{int}K$ and put $m=n+1$. Lift the translated body to the cone $$C=\{(tx,t):t\ge0,\ x\in K-z_0\}\subset\mathbb R^m,
 \qquad D=\{y:\langle y,x\rangle\ge0\text{ for every }x\in C\}.$$ For $V\in\operatorname{int}D$ and $U\in\operatorname{int}C$, set $\chi_C(V)=\int_Ce^{-\langle V,x\rangle}\,dx$ and define $\chi_D(U)$ similarly. Section <a href="#sec:01-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:01-cones">2</a> shows that the desired inequality follows from $\chi_C(V)\chi_D(U)\ge1$ whenever $\langle U,V\rangle=m$. We use positive duality and retain the short calculation to fix the signs and factorials.

The paired maps into $C$ and $D$ arise from Moreau’s decomposition (Moreau 1962; Soltan 2019) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Moreau1962](#ref-Moreau1962), [Soltan2019](#ref-Soltan2019)]. Let $\Pi_C$ and $\Pi_D$ be Euclidean nearest-point projections, and write $Z=\Sigma^{1/2}G$, where $G=(G_1,\ldots,G_m)$ is standard Gaussian and $\Sigma$ is positive definite. For a real layer parameter $z$, choose a bias $\xi_z$ so that $X_z=\Pi_C(Z+\xi_z)$ has mean $a(z)U$, where $a(z)=\mathbb E\max\{g+z,0\}$ for a standard one-dimensional Gaussian $g$. Its dual partner is $Y_z=\Pi_D(-Z-\xi_z)$. Section <a href="#sec:02-projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:02-projections">3</a> proves that every such bias exists and then chooses linear coordinates and the covariance of $Z$ simultaneously. The dependence of the covariance on the projection field is essential to the later matrix cancellation. Integrating the projection maps over $z$ gives two injective, smoothed maps into the cones. Change of variables and Jensen’s inequality turn their Jacobians into a lower bound for the normalized logarithm of the Laplace product in Section <a href="#sec:03-entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:03-entropy">4</a>. Write this lower bound as $-\mathcal E$; the remaining task is to prove $\mathcal E\le0$. Entropy and log-Laplace perturbations also appear in the functional volume-product work of Fradelizi and Marín Sola (Fradelizi and Marín Sola 2026) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[FradeliziMarinSola2024](#ref-FradeliziMarinSola2024)]; that is a related analytical setting, not an input theorem for these maps.

The main difficulty is that the derivative matrices $P_z=D\Pi_C(Z+\xi_z)$ need not commute and need not form an increasing family in $z$. Estimating them independently would discard the information needed for both the constant and equality. Sections <a href="#sec:04-quadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:04-quadratic">5</a> and <a href="#sec:05-layers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:05-layers">6</a> therefore compare the full family to spectral thresholds of a linear Gaussian matrix $L=\sum_{i=1}^mG_iM_i$, where the $M_i$ are deterministic symmetric matrices. Classical Hermite expansion and the Gaussian inverse-generator covariance identity (Houdré and Privault 2002, Proposition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[HoudrePrivault2002](#ref-HoudrePrivault2002)] separate this linear part from higher Gaussian degrees. The resulting upper bound for $\mathcal E$ subtracts a nonnegative layer defect and reduces all other terms to functions of $L$ and the chosen covariance.

Section <a href="#sec:06-linear-equality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:06-linear-equality">7</a> uses the matrix divided-difference formula to express those functions as averages over pairs of eigenvalues. A strict scalar inequality on each nontrivial interval absorbs the remaining errors. Equality then forces the coefficient matrices $M_i$ to commute and the projection derivative to be diagonal in one fixed basis. The cone splits into one-dimensional half-lines, so its bounded section is a simplex. The converse is a direct volume computation.

The dimension-independent estimates used in this chain are stated in Proposition <a href="#prop:scalar-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:scalar-input">5</a>. Appendix <a href="#sec:07-scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:07-scalar">9</a> proves the one-variable and pointwise layer bounds; Appendix <a href="#sec:08-segments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:08-segments">10</a> proves the strict interval inequality. Both include the analytic interpolation and tail arguments that turn their finite rational certificates into statements on the whole real line. Numerical sampling is not used as a substitute for these arguments. Figure <a href="#fig:proof-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-map">1</a> records the logical dependencies and locates the independent scalar branch.

<figure id="fig:proof-map">

<figcaption>Principal logical dependencies. Arrows indicate where inputs are used, rather than the order of presentation. The scalar branch is independent of the cone and dimension; its proofs follow the geometric argument.</figcaption>
</figure>

```text
From the left edge of the top right box, an arrow points diagonally down-left to box 2 ("Normalized Gaussian projections and entropy estimate").
From the vertical downward line between the two right boxes, a horizontal branch with an arrow points left into box 3 ("Quadratic identities and layer error bounds").
From the left edge of the bottom right box, a horizontal arrow points left into box 4 ("Linear-field estimate and equality characterization").
```


<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Functional Mahler inequality

The functional version replaces a convex body by a log-concave function and polarity by convex conjugation. For a proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\varphi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$, define its Fenchel–Legendre transform by $$\varphi^*(y)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb R^n}\{\langle x,y\rangle-\varphi(x)\},
 \qquad y\in\mathbb R^n.$$ We use the convention $e^{-\infty}=0$.

<a id="stmt-cor-functional-mahler"></a>
<div id="cor:functional-mahler" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Functional Mahler inequality). *For every integer $n\ge1$ and every proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\varphi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$ such that $0<\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi(x)}\,dx<\infty$, $$\left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi(x)}\,dx\right)
 \left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi^*(y)}\,dy\right)\ge e^n.$$ The second integral is allowed to be infinite, in which case the inequality is immediate.*

</div>

This sharp lower bound applies to general log-concave functions through their convex potentials; no centering or normalization of $\varphi$ is required. Its derivation uses Theorem <a href="#thm:mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mahler">1</a> in all dimensions, not only in dimension $n$, through the implication of Fradelizi and Meyer (Fradelizi and Meyer 2008, Proposition 2(a)) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[FradeliziMeyer2008](#ref-FradeliziMeyer2008)]. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">8</a> gives the proof, an extremizing example, and the distinction between geometric and functional equality cases.

The same functional bound has an entropy–transport consequence: for full-dimensional log-concave probability measures whose densities vanish at $\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every boundary point of their supports (the essential-continuity condition), it bounds their summed entropies relative to Lebesgue measure by a transport cost between the distributions of their potential gradients, with additive constant $-3n$. The precise definitions and statement appear in Corollary <a href="#cor:entropy-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:entropy-transport">26</a>. This consequence uses the functional equivalence of Gozlan, as stated in (Fradelizi, Gozlan, and Zugmeyer 2021, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[FradeliziGozlanZugmeyer2021](#ref-FradeliziGozlanZugmeyer2021)]; essential continuity is an additional hypothesis, not a requirement on the convex bodies in Theorem <a href="#thm:mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mahler">1</a>.

<a id="sec:01-cones"></a>
### Polarity, cone reduction, and the scalar input

We first express the volume product as a product of Laplace integrals on dual cones. This relation is the one used by Klartag (Klartag 2018, Lemma 2.1 and Corollary 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Klartag2018](#ref-Klartag2018)], with positive duality and a normalization adapted to the Gaussian argument. We include the calculation to fix the signs and factorials. The rest of the proof estimates the integrals after a suitable linear change of coordinates.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The center of polarity

For completeness we establish the minimizing-center convention used in Theorem <a href="#thm:mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mahler">1</a>. The support function of a convex body $K$ is $h_K(u)=\sup_{x\in K}\langle u,x\rangle$. Polar coordinates give, for $z\in\operatorname{int}K$ and $n\ge2$, $$|(K-z)^\circ|=\frac1n\int_{S^{n-1}}
      (h_K(u)-\langle z,u\rangle)^{-n}\,d\sigma(u),$$ where $\sigma$ is surface measure on the unit sphere. This is strictly convex as a function of $z$: the function $t\mapsto t^{-n}$ is strictly convex for $t>0$, and for any two distinct centers their inner products differ on a set of positive surface measure. It also tends to infinity as $z$ approaches the boundary of $K$. Indeed, at a limiting boundary point choose a unit supporting normal $u_0$. Then $\varepsilon=h_K(u_0)-\langle z,u_0\rangle\to0$. The support functions $h_K(u)-\langle z,u\rangle$ have a common Lipschitz constant for $z\in K$. On a spherical cap of radius $\varepsilon$ about $u_0$, their values are at most a constant times $\varepsilon$. The cap has measure at least a constant times $\varepsilon^{n-1}$, so its contribution to the integral is at least a constant times $\varepsilon^{-1}$. Existence and uniqueness of an interior minimizer follow. In dimension one the same conclusion follows directly from $(z-a)^{-1}+(b-z)^{-1}$ for $K=[a,b]$.

For an invertible affine map $x\mapsto Bx+a$, $$(BK+a-(Bz+a))^\circ=B^{-\mathsf T}(K-z)^\circ.$$ The two volume factors change by reciprocal determinants. This proves affine invariance of the volume product and the equivariance $s(BK+a)=Bs(K)+a$.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The cone formulation

Put $m=n+1$. For $z_0\in\operatorname{int}K$, define $$C=\{(y,t):t\ge0,\ y\in t(K-z_0)\},\qquad
D=C^*=\{w:\langle w,x\rangle\ge0\text{ for all }x\in C\}.$$ For cones, the star denotes this *positive* duality. Both cones are closed, convex, solid, and pointed: solid means having nonempty interior, and pointed means containing no line. For interior vectors $V\in D$ and $U\in C$, write $$\chi_C(V)=\int_C e^{-\langle V,x\rangle}\,dx,\qquad
 \chi_D(U)=\int_D e^{-\langle U,x\rangle}\,dx$$ using $m$-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

<a id="stmt-lem-cone-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:cone-reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Cone reduction). *For $U=(0,m)$ and $V=(0,1)$, $$\chi_C(V)\chi_D(U)=\frac{(n!)^2}{m^m}|K|\,|(K-z_0)^\circ|.$$ For any closed convex solid pointed cone and any interior pair $U\in C$, $V\in C^*$, the two Laplace integrals are finite. Their product and the inner product $\langle U,V\rangle$ are preserved by $$(C,D,U,V)\longmapsto(BC,B^{-\mathsf T}D,BU,B^{-\mathsf T}V)$$ for every invertible linear map $B$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cone-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cone-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* A point $(w,t)$ lies in $D$ exactly when $t\ge\sup_{x\in K-z_0}\langle-w,x\rangle$. Since zero is interior to $K-z_0$, this forces $t\ge0$, and the slice at height $t$ is $-t(K-z_0)^\circ$. Integrating the slices gives $$\chi_C((0,1))=n!|K|,\qquad
\chi_D((0,m))=n!m^{-m}|(K-z_0)^\circ|.$$ For general cone data, interiority of $V$ gives $\langle V,x\rangle\ge c|x|$ on $C$, and interiority of $U$ gives the analogous estimate on $D$. These inequalities prove finiteness. Under the displayed transformation, the two change-of-variables factors are $|\det B|$ and $|\det B|^{-1}$, and the inner products in the exponential factors are unchanged. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:1"></a>
It therefore suffices to prove $$\chi_C(V)\chi_D(U)\ \ge\ 1,                            \tag{1}\label{eq:1}$$ whenever $C\subset\mathbb R^m$ is closed, convex, solid, and pointed, $D=C^*$, $U\in\operatorname{int}C$, $V\in\operatorname{int}D$, and $\langle U,V\rangle=m$. These will be the standing cone assumptions. We will also show that equality implies that $C$ is a linear image of an orthant; Section <a href="#sec:06-linear-equality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:06-linear-equality">7</a> completes the equality argument for the original body.

The proof constructs two maps from Gaussians using projections on the cones. To control their Jacobians we compare the whole field of projection derivatives to matrix thresholds, with a linear Gaussian matrix as threshold variable. A small feedback in the Gaussian covariance will be used to handle functions of that linear matrix. In the cone argument $C,D$ refer to cones, while $\mathsf K,\mathsf C$ below are scalar profiles and $\mathbf K,\mathbf C$ will be expectations of their symmetric-matrix evaluations. Real layer indices such as $z$ in the projection field are separate from the Gaussian vector input.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Scalar profiles and their identities

The estimates will use fixed scalar functions, independent of the cone and its dimension. Write $\phi(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-x^2/2}$, $p(x)=\int_{-\infty}^x\phi(y)\,dy$, and $\gamma f=\int f(x)\phi(x)\,dx$. Put $\mathcal N=x\partial_x-\partial_x^2$. To define three profiles, temporarily write $X=p/\phi$, $Y=(1-p)/\phi$, $f=-(1-p)-\log p$, and $j=-p-\log(1-p)$. Set $$d=(1+xX)^2 f+(1-xY)^2 j,\qquad
 g=\tfrac12(1-X^2 f-Y^2 j),\qquad v=\log(XY).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-profile-identities"></a>
<div id="lem:profile-identities" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:2"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Profile identities). *The functions just defined satisfy $$(\mathcal N+2)g=d,\qquad d'=2xd-v'-2g',\qquad
 \mathcal N d-v''=-(\mathcal N+2)(d+g'').                          \tag{2}\label{eq:2}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-profile-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-profile-identities</summary>

*Proof.* We have $X'=1+xX$, $Y'=xY-1$, $f'=-\phi(1-p)/p$, and $j'=\phi p/(1-p)$. Direct substitution gives $$X^2f'+Y^2j'=0,\qquad XX'f'+YY'j'=-1,\qquad
(X')^2f'+(Y')^2j'=-v'.$$ Also $X''=xX'+X$ and $Y''=xY'+Y$. Thus $g'=-(XX'f+YY'j)$, and differentiating once more proves $(\mathcal N+2)g=d$. Differentiating $d=(X')^2f+(Y')^2j$ proves its stated first-order identity. Finally, differentiating the first identity twice gives $d''=(\mathcal N+4)g''$; combining this with the derivative of the second identity proves the third. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The quantitative scalar inequalities
